<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section><section id="sec4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Discussion</h2>
<section id="sec4.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1. Chemical profiling of the leaves and flowers extracts of <em>Lantana camara</em> L. cultivars using UPLC-MS/MS</h3>
<p id="p0215">UPLC-MS is an important tool utilized to unveil the differences in metabolic profiles among different <em>L. camara</em> cultivars (<a href="#bib75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib75">Soininen et al., 2014</a>). MZmine software (2.53) was used for the extraction of the retention time, peak area, and MS/MS intensity of components from raw UPLC- MS/MS data.</p>
<p id="p0220">Heat map (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>) was constructed using Gitools program (<a href="http://www.gitools.org/download" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.gitools.org/download</a>) based on the total ion current values of the identified mass fragments. This heat map illustrates the relative amounts of the identified metabolites in each sample, and this is represented with varying color intensity from green (minimum quantity) to red (maximum quantity).</p>
<p id="p0225">The identified metabolites belong to different chemical classes. Investigation of their chromatographic patterns and characteristic fragmentation pathways formerly illustrated in literature enabled to gain as much structural information as possible.</p>
<section id="sec4.1.1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.1. Triterpenes</h4>
<p id="p0230">Nineteen peaks (<strong>24, 30–47</strong>) were identified as triterpenes, among which 2 peaks (<strong>24</strong> and <strong>30</strong>) belong to euphane-type triterpene (<a href="#bib54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib54">Kikuchi et al., 2016</a>). Compound <strong>24</strong> showed a quasi-molecular peak (M-H) at 775 Da, characteristic mass fragments at 613 Da (M-H-162) and 715 Da (M-H-120) due to loss of glucose unit and two acetate groups, respectively and it was tentatively identified as 3α,30-Diacetyloxy-12α-hydroxy-23-oxoeupha-7,24-dien-21,16<em>β</em>-olid-28-oic acid 28-O-<em>β</em>-D-glucopyranosyl ester. Furthermore, compound <strong>30</strong> showed a quasi-molecular ion peak at 555 Da and primarily yielded daughter ions at 495 Da (M-H-60), 511 Da (M-H-44), 537 Da (M-H-18) and 540 Da (M-H-15) due to the loss of acetate, CO<sub>2</sub>, water and methyl group, respectively. This compound was tentatively identified as 3-acetyloxy,12-hydroxy-23-oxoeupha-7,24-dien-21,16-olid-28-oic acid. Peak <strong>44</strong> exhibited a lupane-type triterpene nucleus and it showed characteristic mass fragments at 425 Da (M-H-CO<sub>2</sub>), 451 Da (M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O) (<a href="#bib8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib8">Ayatollahi et al., 2011</a>). The corresponding compound was characterized as lantabetulic acid. The existence of significantly important daughter ions due to loss of angeloyl or methyl butenoyl or hydroxyl moiety followed by loss of CO<sub>2</sub> were displayed in the mass spectra of nine peaks (<strong>31, 32, 33, 35, 39, 40, 41, 45</strong> and <strong>47</strong>) belonging to oleane-type triterpenoids. By referring to literature, they are tentatively identified as, camarolic acid, camaric acid, lantanillic acid, icterogenin, reduced lantadene A, 3-O-(3-methyl-2-butenoyl), 22-hydroxy-12-oleanen-28-oic acid, lantadene B, lantanolic acid and camardienone, respectively (<a href="#bib8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib8">Ayatollahi et al., 2011</a>; <a href="#bib14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib14">Begum et al., 2008</a>, <a href="#bib13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib13">2014</a>; <a href="#bib21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib21">Chen et al., 2010</a>). Finally, 7 peaks (peaks <strong>34, 36, 37, 38, 42, 43</strong> and <strong>46</strong>) were found to belong to ursane-type triterpenes. The most important mass fragments were due to loss of angeloyl or methyl butenoyl or hydroxyl moiety followed by loss of CO<sub>2</sub>. These compounds were tentatively identified as lantacin, lantoic acid, camaranoic acid, camarinic acid, lantiursolic acid and lantic acid, respectively (<a href="#bib8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib8">Ayatollahi et al., 2011</a>; <a href="#bib14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib14">Begum et al., 2008</a>, <a href="#bib13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib13">2014</a>; <a href="#bib21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib21">Chen et al., 2010</a>).</p></section><section id="sec4.1.2"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.2. Flavonoids</h4>
<p id="p0235">Eight compounds were identified as flavonoids; among which 5 were identified as flavone glycosides (<strong>peaks 1, 14, 21, 22, 23</strong>) and 3 as methoxylated flavones (<strong>peaks 25, 26, 29</strong>). Compounds <strong>1</strong> and <strong>14</strong> were recognized to be diglycosides. Compound <strong>1</strong> was annotated as diglucoside from the MS<sup>2</sup> fragments at (M-H-162) and (M-H-162) that indicated the loss of two hexose units connected to two different positions of the aglycone (<a href="#bib17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib17">Brito et al., 2014</a>). Furthermore, the MS<sup>2</sup> fragment at 429 Da indicated that the inter-glycosidic linkage was (1–2) linkage (<a href="#bib49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib49">Kachlicki et al., 2016</a>). Meanwhile, the fragmentation pattern of compound <strong>14</strong> showed two characteristic MS<sup>2</sup> fragments (M-H-162) and (M-H-162-162) that indicated the loss of two sugar units at different substitution position of the aglycone unit (<a href="#bib38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib38">Ghallab et al., 2020</a>). Compounds <strong>21, 22</strong> and <strong>23</strong> were deduced to be monoglycosides due to the presence of the MS<sup>2</sup> fragment (M-H-162) indicating the loss of a hexose unit (<a href="#bib49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib49">Kachlicki et al., 2016</a>).</p>
<p id="p0240">The aglycone part of flavonoids can be identified based on further MS<sup>2</sup> experiments with CE = 70 eV. Compound <strong>1</strong> showed characteristic mass fragments at 137 and 151 Da resulting from RDA (Retro-Diels Alder rearrangement) in addition to a mass fragment at 241 Da (M-H-CO<sub>2</sub>). Based on these observations and by referring to the literature survey, it was tentatively identified as luteolin-7, 4'-O-diglucoside (<a href="#bib57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib57">Li et al., 2016</a>). Compound <strong>14</strong> was identified as a di-methoxylated flavone, as deduced from the MS<sup>2</sup> fragment of its aglycone (M-H-2CH<sub>3</sub>) at 299 Da (<a href="#bib88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib88">Zhao et al., 2020</a>). After comprehensive analysis of the mass data with that reported in literature, compound <strong>14</strong> was identified as pectolinarin (<a href="#bib58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib58">Liao et al., 2018</a>). Compounds <strong>21, 22</strong> and <strong>23</strong> exhibited a di-methoxylated flavone glucoside skeleton as all of them showed a mass fragment (M-H-2CH<sub>3</sub>) at 299 Da. Compound <strong>21</strong> was tentatively identified as dihydroxy-dimethoxy-flavone hexoside I, showing characteristic RDA fragments at 181 Da (MeA <sup>1,3</sup>) and at 150 Da (MeB<sup>−0,2</sup>). That confirmed the presence of one methoxy and two hydroxy groups at ring A in addition to one methoxy and one hydroxy group in ring B. Meanwhile, compounds <strong>22</strong> and <strong>23</strong> were tentatively identified as dihydroxy-dimethoxy-flavone hexoside II and III displaying characteristic RDA fragments at 165 Da (MeA<sup>−1,3</sup>), 150 Da (MeB<sup>−0,2</sup>) and 181 Da (MeA<sup>−1,3</sup>), 134 Da (MeB<sup>−0,2</sup>), respectively. Those findings indicated that compound <strong>22</strong> possessed one methoxy and one hydroxy substituent in both rings A and ring B. On the other hand, compound <strong>23</strong> demonstrated a different substitution pattern with one methoxy and two hydroxy groups in ring A in addition to one methoxy group in ring B. Peaks <strong>25, 26</strong> and <strong>30</strong> corresponded to mono, di and tri-methoxylated flavone aglycones, respectively. This was inferred by the presence of the characteristic mass fragment 300Da (M-H-CH<sub>3</sub>) for compound <strong>25</strong>, 299 Da (M-H-2CH<sub>3</sub>) for compound <strong>26</strong> and at 282 Da (M-H-3CH<sub>3</sub>) for compound <strong>29</strong> (<a href="#bib88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib88">Zhao et al., 2020</a>).Compound <strong>25</strong> showed characteristic RDA fragments at 181 Da (MeA<sup>−1,3</sup>) and136 Da (B<sup>−1,3</sup>), while characteristic peaks of compound 27 were at 195 Da (2MeA<sup>−1,3</sup>) and 136 Da (B<sup>−1,3</sup>). Moreover, the RDA fragments of compound <strong>29</strong> appeared at 195 Da (2MeA<sup>−1,3</sup>) and 134 Da (Me B<sup>−1,3</sup>). Based on the above-mentioned information and by referring to literature, compounds <strong>25, 26</strong> and <strong>29</strong> were tentatively identified as eupafolin (<a href="#bib44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib44">Huang et al., 2020</a>), crisiliol (<a href="#bib60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib60">Moharram et al., 2012</a>) and salvigenin (<a href="#bib58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib58">Liao et al., 2018</a>), respectively.</p></section><section id="sec4.1.3"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.3. Phenolic acids</h4>
<p id="p0245">Peaks <strong>2, 3, 6, 8</strong> and <strong>9</strong> were annotated as phenolic acids that showed a characteristic peak due to the loss of 44 Da (M-H-CO<sub>2</sub>), though, loss of 15 Da (M-H-CH<sub>3</sub>) was also noticed in mass spectrum of peak <strong>6</strong>. By referring to literature, compounds <strong>2, 3, 6, 8</strong> and <strong>9</strong> were tentatively identified as protocatechuic acid, caffeic acid, vanillic acid, coumaric acid (<a href="#bib55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib55">Kumar et al., 2017</a>) and ferulic acid (<a href="#bib12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib12">Barros et al., 2012</a>), respectively.</p></section><section id="sec4.1.4"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.4. Iridoid glycosides</h4>
<p id="p0250">Three peaks (<strong>5, 11</strong> and <strong>12)</strong> were identified to be iridoid glycosides. All of them showed the characteristic peak (M + HCOO–H) and all of these compounds showed their major MS<sup>2</sup> fragment (M-H). The formate anion (M + HCOO–H) is commonly resulted from iridoid glycosides bearing an ester group or a carboxyl group at C-4 (<a href="#bib70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib70">Ren et al., 2007</a>). Comparing to literature values, compound <strong>5</strong> was tentatively identified as theveside displaying characteristic peaks at 371 Da, 345 Da and 209 Da owing to loss of water, CO<sub>2</sub> and glucose moieties, respectively (<a href="#bib70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib70">Ren et al., 2007</a>). Compounds <strong>11</strong> and <strong>12</strong> showed their characteristic peaks as a result of losing methoxy group (M-H-30) at 391 Da and 359 Da, respectively. Furthermore, there were fragments due to loss of glucose unit (M-H-162) from the precursor ion at 259 Da and 227 Da, respectively along with a methyl ester loss represented by the mass fragment (M - H - 60) at 361 Da and 329 Da, respectively with subsequent dehydration to afford the major product ion (M - H - 78) at 343 Da and 311 Da, respectively. Based on this information and by referring to literature, compounds <strong>11</strong> and <strong>12</strong> were tentatively identified to be lamiridoside and 8-epiloganin, respectively (<a href="#bib18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib18">Calixto et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26">Czerwińska et al., 2020</a>).</p></section><section id="sec4.1.5"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.5. Phenylethanoid glycosides</h4>
<p id="p0255">Seven peaks; <strong>4, 7, 16, 17, 18, 19</strong> and <strong>20</strong> were identified as phenylethanoid glycosides. This chemical class characterized by a directly attached <em>β</em>-glucopyranose to hydroxyphenyl ethyl moiety. Substitution by hydroxyl derivatives of cinnamic acid (such as caffeoyl and feruloyl) usually occurs at the positions C-4 and C-6. Another sugar moiety is usually located at the C-2 or C-3 position of <em>β</em>-glucopyranose (<a href="#bib86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib86">Xue and Yang, 2016</a>). Regarding compounds <strong>16, 17, 18, 19</strong> and <strong>20</strong>, the presence of two highly abundant mass fragments at 161 and 179 Da indicated the presence of caffeoyl moiety attached to the glucose unit (<a href="#bib56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib56">Li et al., 2014</a>). Moreover, parent ions were subjected to three sequential losses of caffeoyl moiety, deoxyhexose (rhamnose) moiety (M-H-162-146) (<a href="#bib56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib56">Li et al., 2014</a>) and glucose unit followed by dehydration to yield another daughter fragment (M-H-162-146-162-18) at 135 Da assigned for anhydrophenolethanol moiety (<a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7">Attia et al., 2018</a>). The same fragmentation pattern was also noticed for compounds <strong>4</strong> and <strong>7</strong>, however, they possessed two pentose units - rather than deoxyhexose attached to the glucose moiety, thus, two characteristic fragments appeared; the first at 315 Da (M-H-162-132-132 -162) (<a href="#bib56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib56">Li et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7">Attia et al., 2018</a>) resulting from the successive loss of caffeoyl, two pentose and glucose units. The second appeared at 135 Da (M-H-162-132-132-162-18) was owing to further loss of water moiety. Based on the mentioned information and by referring to literature, compounds <strong>4, 7, 16, 17, 18, 19</strong> and were tentatively identified as fucatoside B (<a href="#bib48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib48">Juliao et al., 2009</a>), fucatoside C (<a href="#bib48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib48">Juliao et al., 2009</a>), arenarioside (<a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39">Gong et al., 2020</a>), verbascoside (<a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7">Attia et al., 2018</a>), cis-verbascoside (<a href="#bib6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib6">Ashour, 2021</a>), isoverbascoside (<a href="#bib6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib6">Ashour, 2021</a>) and Parvifloroside A (<a href="#bib48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib48">Juliao et al., 2009</a>), respectively.</p></section><section id="sec4.1.6"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.6. Lignan glycosides</h4>
<p id="p0260">One peak was identified to be a lignan glycoside (Peaks <strong>15</strong>). It showed two characteristic peaks at 423 Da (M-H- 162) and 570 Da (M-H-15) due to loss of glucose and methyl group, respectively. This compound was identified as radulignan (<a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">Filho et al., 2009</a>).</p></section><section id="sec4.1.7"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.7. Miscellaneous compounds</h4>
<p id="p0265">Compound <strong>10</strong> presented a deprotonated ion peak at 45 Da (M-H-galactose) and it was tentatively identified as ethyl-<em>β</em>-D-galactoside (<a href="#bib59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib59">Misra et al., 2007</a>). The tentatively identified linaloic acid hexoside (menthiafolic acid hexoside, peak <strong>13</strong>) was successively dehydrated to afford fragments at 327 Da (M-H-H<sub>2</sub>O) and 309 Da (M-H-2H<sub>2</sub>O) provided additional fragments at 183 Da (M-H-162) due to hexose loss (<a href="#bib40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib40">Goodger and Woodrow, 2013</a>). Compound <strong>27</strong> was recognized as a naphthoquinone derivative and this was deduced from its characteristic fragments at305 Da (M-H-CO), 277 Da (M-H–CO–CO) in addition to a fragment due to the loss of methyl group (M-H-CH<sub>3</sub>) (<a href="#bib34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib34">Elwoo et al., 1970</a>) and by referring to literature it was tentatively identified as lantalucratin F (<a href="#bib43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib43">Hayash et al., 2004</a>). Finally, compound <strong>28</strong> was identified as furanonaphthoquinone derivative, representing characteristic fragments (M-H-CO) and (M-H–CO–CO) (<a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30">Diaz and Medina, 1996</a>) and by referring to literature it was identified as 5-hydroxynaphtho[2,3-b]furan-4,9-dione (<a href="#bib2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib2">Abeygunawardena et al., 1991</a>).</p></section></section><section id="sec4.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.2. Investigation of the inter-cultivar effect on the metabolic profile using multivariate statistical analysis of the UPLC-MS/MS data</h3>
<section id="sec4.2.1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.2.1. Unsupervised pattern recognition</h4>
<p id="p0270">The UPLC-MS/MS data matrix was subjected to the unsupervised pattern recognition, principal component analysis (PCA) (<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>A) in order to explore the clustering pattern of different <em>Lantana</em> cultivars extracts based on their chemical profiles. The first two principal components (PC1, PC2) represent 55% of total variation within all samples. The model was valid as it showed R2X (cum) value of 0.98 indicating the goodness of fit of the model. Its predictive power was significantly high as indicated by the Q2 (cum) value of 0.95.</p>
<p id="p0275">The loading plot (<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>B) showed the distribution of chemical constituents among different hydroalcoholic extracts of <em>L. camara</em> cultivars. Therefore, it can identify the chemical markers that are responsible for samples segregation.</p></section></section><section id="sec4.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.3. Cytotoxicity and anti-COVID activity of the tested extracts</h3>
<section id="sec4.3.1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.3.1. Assessment of the cytotoxicity of the tested extracts on Vero E6 cells</h4>
<p id="p0280">MTT assay was used to assess the effect of the tested extracts on the viability of Vero E6 cells (Green monkeys kidney cells) (<a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>).</p></section><section id="sec4.3.2"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.3.2. Anti-COVID-19 activity using plaque reduction assay</h4>
<p id="p0285">The antiviral activity of the tested extracts against (COVID-19) beta (B.1.1.3.) strains was evaluated using plaque reduction assay. IC<sub>50</sub> values were determined using Graphpad Instat software (GraphPad Software Inc, California) by plotting % reduction against different concentrations of each tested extract (<a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). In order to ensure the safety and efficacy of the tested extracts, the selectivity indices that measures the window between the cytotoxicity and antiviral activity (<a href="#bib46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib46">Indrayanto et al., 2021</a>) were calculated based on CC<sub>50</sub> values obtained from MTT test and IC<sub>50</sub> values of plaque reduction assay. The higher the selectivity index, the safer and the more effective the drug is (<a href="#bib46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib46">Indrayanto et al., 2021</a>).</p></section><section id="sec4.3.3"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.3.3. Effects of the tested extracts on expression of SARS-CoV-2 RdRp and E genes</h4>
<p id="p0290">The promising safety and antiviral activity of the aforementioned extracts have triggered the need to deeply understand the possible mechanisms of their anti-COVID-19 activity. This was achieved by estimating the transcript levels of RdRp and E genes using a TaqMan Real-time RT-PCR assay. Although coronavirus has many viral proteins, many studies showed and proved that RdRp is the most relevant viral target to identify specific anti-COVID-19 agents, as it is considered as a vital enzyme for the life cycle of RNA viruses because it is responsible for viral RNA replication in host cells which lack an enzymatic counterpart (<a href="#bib87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib87">Zhao et al., 2021</a>). In fact, viral polymerases inhibitors represent the cornerstone of antiviral therapeutics; indeed, most of the approved drugs for the treatment of viral infections, including HIV and HCV, belong to this class (<a href="#bib20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib20">Cannalire et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib25">Cui et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib32">Elfiky, 2020</a>; <a href="#bib84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib84">Wu et al., 2020</a>). On the other hand, E gene encodes a small and significant structural protein for coronaviruses which in turn contributes to many vital processes of coronaviruses, such as assembly, budding, and envelope formation making it an important target for anti-COVID-19 drugs (<a href="#bib76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib76">Sun et al., 2020</a>). Four levels of extracts' safe concentrations (30, 15, 12.5, 6.25 μg/mL) were plotted against the % down regulation of RdRp and E genes (<a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>).</p></section></section><section id="sec4.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.4. Determination of the anti-COVID-19 biomarkers using supervised pattern recognition analysis</h3>
<p id="p0295">In order to investigate the clustering of the tested extracts based on their anti-COVID-19 activity in addition to determination of the biomarkers that are responsible for such activity, UPLC-MS/MS data matrix (X-variables) and IC<sub>50</sub> values obtained from plaque reduction assay (Y-variables) were imported to Orthogonal Projection to Latent Structure (OPLS) as one of supervised pattern recognition models. The OPLS model showed goodness of fit (R<sup>2</sup>) value of 0.99 and good predictive power (Q2) value of 0.97 indicating the validity of the model (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>).</p>
<p id="p0300">Coefficient plot (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>) was constructed to determine the metabolites which had positive contribution to the anti-COVID-19 activity of the studied extracts. It is interesting to note that twelve biomarkers (positive contributors) associated with COVID-19 inhibitory activity were determined. These biomarkers were found in high concentration in the most active extracts as following: camaric acid, lantanilic acid, lantiursolic acid, luteolin-7,4'-O diglucoside, lantabetulic acid, camarolic acid, icterogenin, lantaoic acid, camaranoic acid, lantacin and lantic acid were present in the highest concentration in <em>L. camara</em> cv. Chelsea gem leaves extract. Meanwhile, ferulic acid was detected in a high concentration in <em>L. camara</em> cv. Spreading sunset flower extract, while isoverbascoside scored the highest concentration in the leaves extract of <em>L. camara</em> cv. Drap d'or. This could explain the remarkable contribution of these biomarkers to the stunning anti-COVID-19 potentials of such extracts that ultimately discriminated them from cluster B extracts (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>). By referring to literature, the identified biomarkers were found to have antiviral activity. Ferulic acid exhibited antiviral activity against plant viruses and H1N1 virus (<a href="#bib41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib41">Hariono et al., 2016</a>; <a href="#bib82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib82">Wang et al., 2017</a>). Many studies showed that ursane and oleane type triterpenes possessed antiviral activity mainly against respiratory viruses (<a href="#bib22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib22">Chen et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib47">Jesus et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#bib53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib53">Khwaza et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#bib66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib66">Pawełczyk and Zaprutko, 2020</a>; <a href="#bib78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib78">Tohmé et al., 2019</a>). Moreover, a previous study revealed that isoverbascoside possessed a high binding affinity to SARS-CoV-2 (<a href="#bib9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib9">Banerjee et al., 2021a</a>). Furthermore, luteolin was proved to have antiviral activity against different viral strains (<a href="#bib65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib65">Ninfali et al., 2020</a>).</p></section><section id="sec4.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.5. Molecular docking studies on the biomarkers</h3>
<p id="p0305">RdRp (NSP12) plays its crucial role in the viral transcription cycle with the help of cofactors NSP7 and NSP8 that are present in its crystal structure. Its active site is tunnel shaped cavity located between the NiRAN (nucleotidyltransferase) domain β-hairpin constituting 398–919 amino acid residues, in which the catalytic residues were localized (<a href="#bib79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib79">Vardhan and Sahoo, 2020a</a>). RdRp hydrophobic cavities at active N and C-terminals are involved in catalysis of RNA polymerization (<a href="#bib80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib80">Vardhan and Sahoo, 2020b</a>). The catalytic active site of the RdRp consists of seven conserved motifs (A to G); The RNA template is expected to enter the active site composed of motifs A and C through a groove clamped by motifs F and G. The NTP (nucleotide triphosphate) entry channel is formed by a set of hydrophilic residues, including LYS545, ARG553, ARG555 and ASN691 in motif F that are predicted to play roles in drug interactions (<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1">Abd El-Aziz et al., 2021</a>). Strong electrostatic surfaces are observed via divalent cationic residues 611–626, especially the residue ASP618. Some others are also located between residues 753–769 (<a href="#bib79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib79">Vardhan and Sahoo, 2020a</a>), hence, these residues Asp618, Ser759, Asp760, Asp761, Asp623, Arg555, Arg553, Lys545, Val557, Ser682, Asn691 and Thr680 were used for receptor grid generation and screening of compounds.</p>
<p id="p0310">Since RdRp was previously observed as the main target of action for the tested extracts’ anti-COVID activity, hence; the predetermined biomarkers were docked into its active site in order to investigate the possible interaction patterns and afford deep understanding to the molecular mechanisms of action of these molecules. For the screening purposes, remdesivir was used as an example of FDA approved drugs for comparative analysis (<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1">Abd El-Aziz et al., 2021</a>).</p>
<p id="p0315">The molecular interaction analysis of these top hit compounds with SARS-CoV-2 RdRp revealed that they were involved in interactions with most of the aforementioned amino acid residues confirming that they were successfully docked into the RdRp binding site (<a href="#bib5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib5">Aouidate et al., 2021</a>).</p>
<p id="p0320">The docked pose of remdesivir against RdRp was represented in <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Figure (6)</a>
. Herein, it was found that it fitted well into the active site via forming hydrogen bonds with Asp761, Tyr619, Arg553, Cys622 as well as negatively charged interactions with Asp623, Asp618, Asp760 and Asp761. Positively charged interactions with Lys621, Arg624, Arg553, Arg555 and Lys545 alongside, hydrophobic interactions with Trp617, Glu813, Leu758, Cys622, Tyr619, Tyr455 and Val557 were also observed. Remdesivir also bound to the core protein through polar interactions with the amino acid residues: Ser759, Ser814, Thr556, Ser682; one salt bridge and another pi-pi interaction with Arg553. These interactions were in close agreement to those reported in literature which proved their important role in enzyme catalysis (<a href="#bib77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib77">Tiwari, 2021</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig6" title="Fig. 6"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8830149_gr6_lrg.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa15/8830149/4cc405dd6f86/gr6_lrg.jpg" loading="lazy" height="316" width="740" alt="Fig. 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>2D (A) and 3D (B) ligand interaction diagrams for docking poses of the known FDA approved anti-viral remdesivir in the active site of SARS-CoV-2 RdRp crystalline structure (PBD ID 6M71).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0325">Comparing the 2D interaction diagrams of the top hit compounds: isoverbascoside, luteolin-7, 4'-O -diglucoside, camarolic acid and lantoic acid (<a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>, <a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>
) to that of remdesivir demonstrated common positive charged interactions with the amino acids Arg553 and Arg555 that constitute part of this polymerase NTP channel structure. That signified their crucial role in the enzyme inhibition via preventing the entry of adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and divalent cations into the central active site cavity, thus attenuating RNA replication (<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1">Abd El-Aziz et al., 2021</a>). However, isoverbascoside was involved in ten H-bonds (Arg624, Thr556, Tyr619, Asp618, Asp760, Lys621 and Ser795); luteolin-7, 4'-O-diglucoside was involved in seven H-bonds (Asp760, Asp761, Ser682, Thr556, Lys621, Asp619); camarolic acid was incorporated in six H-bonds (Arg55, Arg553, Lys545, Asp452 and Thr556) and lantoic acid was engaged in four hydrogen bonds (Asn691, Arg555, Arg553 and Lys551) compared to remdesivir (exhibiting only three bonds with Asp761, Tyr619 and Arg553). As a result, better interaction (as expressed by their docking GScores) and stronger inhibition of the SARS-CoV-2 RdRp was achieved.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig7" title="Fig. 7"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8830149_gr7_lrg.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa15/8830149/1d156f241dc7/gr7_lrg.jpg" loading="lazy" height="392" width="740" alt="Fig. 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>2D (A) and 3D (B) ligand interaction diagrams for docking poses of isoverbascoside and luteolin −7, 4'-O -diglucoside in the active site of SARS-CoV-2 RdRp crystalline structure (PBD ID 6M71).</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig8" title="Fig. 8"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8830149_gr8_lrg.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/aa15/8830149/21f336d96316/gr8_lrg.jpg" loading="lazy" height="374" width="740" alt="Fig. 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>2D (A) and 3D (B) ligand interaction diagrams for docking poses of camarolic acid and lantoic acid in the active site of SARS-CoV-2 RdRp crystalline structure (PBD ID 6M71).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0330">In addition to the highest number of hydrogen bonding interactions displayed by isoverbascoside, it was engaged in more negative and positive charged interactions with the key amino acid residues (<a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>), leading to formation of the most stable complex within the active site of the RdRp protein, hence; the highest Gscore value.</p>
<p id="p0335">From <a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>, <a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>, it has been postulated that luteolin- 7, 4'-O-diglucoside had the tendency to form a more conformationally fitting complex than camarolic and lantoic acids through the formation of higher number of hydrogen bonds, hydrophobic and negatively charged interactions, thus might contribute significantly to its higher <em>in-silico</em> COVID-19 inhibitory activity.</p>
<p id="p0340">Camarolic and lantoic acids are considered as examples of oleanane and ursane type pentacyclic triterpenes, respectively, that share many structural similarities including a C-28 carboxylic acid and two hydroxyls at C-33 and C-22. However, it seemed that substituents at the hydroxy group of C-22 such as 3-hydroxy-2-methylene butanoyl moieties were notably integrated in favourable interactions with the enzyme. For instance, both compounds shared the same number of H-bonds via their carboxylic group at C-28, however camarolic acid was observed to engage in extra four H-bonds with the surface accessible residues Arg553, Asp452 and Thr556, unlike lantoic acid, which lacked the 3-hydroxy-2-methylene butanoyl moiety at C-22. This in turn limited the number of hydrogen bonds formed to only one with Lys551(<a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>).</p>
<p id="p0345">Recent studies have reported the <em>in-vitro</em> inhibitory activity of isoverbascoside to SARS-CoV-2 main protease (<a href="#bib9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib9">Banerjee et al., 2021a</a>) in addition to its antiviral activity against respiratory syncytial virus (<a href="#bib52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib52">Kernan et al., 1998</a>). Literature lacks accurate information regarding the efficacy of luteolin-7,4'-O-diglucoside and lantoic acid as anti-viral drugs, while promising anti-viral activity of camarolic acid as a pentacyclic oleanolic acid triterpene was previously noted (<a href="#bib11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib11">Banik and Pandey, 2008</a>). As far as we know, no inhibitory activity to SARS-CoV-2 RdRp was reported for such compounds. This triggers the critical need for their targeted isolation coupled with extensive <em>in-vitro</em> and <em>in-vivo</em> studies to afford deep explanation and verification of their promising <em>in-silico</em> activities. These comprehensive studies might be incorporated in presenting new COVID-19 inhibitors which have not previously reported for this activity.</p></section></section><section id="sec5"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusion</h2>
<p id="p0350">In conclusion, extracts of four <em>L. camara</em> cultivars were recognized as rich sources of health promoting phytoconstituents for the human being especially in attenuating COVID-19 virus as a newly reported activity that was not previously reported despite the plant’s historical role in alleviating respiratory ailments. The combination of a multiplatform metabolite profiling using UPLC-MS/MS and chemometrics is a promising approach to afford deep understanding to the chemical composition differences among the cultivars extracts and correlate them to COVID-19 inhibitory activities allowing to pinpoint possible candidate metabolites responsible for the biological activity of the extracts. Since molecular docking studies confirmed the promising activities of isoverbascoside, luteolin-7, 4'-O-diglucoside, camarolic and lantoic acids, our future perspective will focus on isolation of these biomarkers, and subjecting them to extensive <em>in-vitro, in-vivo</em> and clinical studies aiming to provide verification to their promising <em>in-silico</em> activities.</p></section><section id="sec6"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Authors’ contribution</h2>
